First, let's decode the exact sphere_intersect math into Python.

sphere_intersect(ray origin o[3], dir d[3], center c[3], radius r):
- s14=o0, s13=o1, s12=o2, s10=d0, s11=d1, s9=d2.
- s4 = o1 - c1
- s7 = o0 - c0
- s6 = o2 - c2
- s0 = (o-c)·(o-c) - r^2 = s4^2+s7^2+s6^2 - r^2? Wait it computes:
  400e74: s0 = s4*s4
  400e80: s0 += s7*s7
  400e90: s0 += s6*s6
  400e9c: fmsub s0,s3,s3,s0  => s0 = r*r - (len2)? `fmsub d, dn, dm, da` computes d*n - m? Actually AArch64 `fmsub Sd, Sn, Sm, Sa` = Sd = Sn*Sm - Sa. So s0 = s3*s3 - s0 = r^2 - |o-c|^2. Hmm need sign.
  Later uses discriminant:
  - s15 = d·d = s11^2+s10^2+s9^2 (using s11,s10,s9)
  - s4 = d·(o-c) = s4*s11 + s7*s10 + s6*s9
  - s0 = r^2 - |o-c|^2
  - s3 = s15*4 ? At 400ea0: `fmul s3, s15, s5` where s5=4.0 => s3=4*s15.
  - s3 = s3 * s0 => 4*s15*(r^2-|o-c|^2)
  - s4 = s4+s4 = 2*d·(o-c)
  - s3 = fnmsub s3, s4, s4, s3 => s3 = s4*s4 - s3? `fnmsub` = -(Sn*Sm) + Sa? Actually `fnmsub Sd, Sn, Sm, Sa` computes Sd = -(Sn * Sm) + Sa. So s3 = s4^2 - 4*s15*(r^2-|o-c|^2) = discriminant B^2 - 4AC where B=2 d·(o-c), A=s15, C=|o-c|^2-r^2? Let's derive standard quadratic.

Standard ray-sphere: |o + t d - c|^2 = r^2
=> (d·d) t^2 + 2 d·(o-c) t + (|o-c|^2 - r^2) = 0
A = d·d = s15
B = 2 d·(o-c)
C = |o-c|^2 - r^2
Disc = B^2 - 4AC.

But code uses s0 = r^2 - |o-c|^2 = -C. And s3 = 4*s15*s0 = -4AC. Then s3 = B^2 - (-4AC)? `fnmsub s3,s4,s4,s3` = -(B*B) + s3 = s3 - B^2 = -4AC - B^2, which is negative discriminant. Then it checks `fcmpe s3,#0.0; b.mi` if s3<0 => miss. But standard disc = B^2-4AC would be positive for hit. Here s3 = -disc. Hmm maybe the sign is different due to C.

Let's recompute: s3 initial = -4AC. After fnmsub: s3 = -(B^2) + s3 = -B^2 - 4AC = -(B^2+4AC). That's not standard.

Let's map constants precisely. At sphere_intersect:
- s0 = s3*s3 - s0 where s0 initially was |o-c|^2? Wait:
  - 400e74: s0 = s4*s4
  - 400e80: s0 += s7*s7
  - 400e90: s0 += s6*s6
  So s0 = |o-c|^2.
  - 400e9c: `fmsub s0, s3, s3, s0` => s0 = r^2 - |o-c|^2. Yes C_neg = r^2 - |o-c|^2 = -C.
- s4 = d·(o-c) = s7*s10 + s4*s11 + s6*s9.
- s15 = |d|^2 = s11^2+s10^2+s9^2.
- At 400ea0: s3 = s15 * 4 = 4A.
- s3 = s3 * s0 = 4A*(r^2-|o-c|^2) = -4AC.
- s4 = s4+s4 = B.
- fnmsub s3,s4,s4,s3 = -(B*B) + (-4AC) = -(B^2 + 4AC). Not standard.

That would be wrong. Maybe `fnmsub` semantics different: `FNMSUB` = "Floating-point Negated Multiply-Subtract": Sd = -(Sn * Sm) + Sa? Let's verify AArch64 instruction names:
- `FMADD Sd, Sn, Sm, Sa`: Sd = Sn*Sm + Sa
- `FMSUB Sd, Sn, Sm, Sa`: Sd = Sn*Sm - Sa
- `FNMADD`: Sd = -(Sn*Sm) + Sa? Actually "FN" prefix means negate product; and "SUB" means subtract addend? Let's recall:
  - FMADD: d = n*m + a
  - FMSUB: d = n*m - a
  - FNMADD: d = -(n*m) + a  (i.e., a - n*m)
  - FNMSUB: d = -(n*m) - a  (i.e., -(n*m + a))
From ARM docs: `FNMSUB Vd.4S, Vn.4S, Vm.4S, Va.4S` -> "Floating-point Negated Multiply-Subtract: Vd = -(Vn*Vm) - Va". Hmm not sure Pascal.

Let's test with disassembly: At 400eac `fnmsub s3, s4, s4, s3`. If semantics = -(s4*s4) - s3, then s3 = -B^2 - (-4AC) = -B^2 + 4AC = 4AC - B^2 = -disc? That would be negative discriminant, and miss if s3<0 -> hit if disc>0. That works! Because s3 = 4AC - B^2 = -disc. Branch if s3 < 0 -> hit (disc>0). Hmm but branch at 400eb0 is `b.mi 400f90` (miss) if s3 < 0. If s3 = 4AC - B^2, then for a hit (disc>0), s3<0, branch to miss — that contradicts. Let's compute actual numbers with a known geometry.

Let's implement both interpretations and test against actual sphere_intersect output for a known ray to see if hit flag matches. We can emulate? Better: write small C using original formula reconstructing root values and compare to image? Too much. Let's derive from sphere_intersect later root computation:
At hit path:
- s4 (B) is used:
  - 400ebc `fneg s4, s4` => s4 = -B
  - For discriminant d3 (double of s3), if d3 == 0: `fmov d0,d3` (0) then `sqrt` -> 0; s5 = (-B - 0)/ (2*s15) = -B/(2A)
  - If d3 > 0 (b.pl): s5 = (-B - sqrt(d3))/(2A)? At 400f78: `fsub d5,d4,d3` then divide => (-B - sqrt(disc))/(2A). Wait d4 = fcvt(-B) double, d3 = sqrt(disc), d15 = 2A. So t = (-B - sqrt(disc))/(2A). But 400ec4 branches `b.pl 400f60` if d3 > 0, taking `-B - sqrt`; if d3 == 0 falls through 400ecc uses sqrt(d3)=0 then `d5 = d4 - d5` = -B - 0. So same value. So t = (-B - sqrt(disc))/(2A). There is no +sqrt option because the nearest root chosen is the smaller t? Wait subtracting sqrt gives the smaller root if B>0; standard roots are (-B±√disc)/(2A) for A t^2 + B t + C. The code uses (-B - √disc)/(2A) which is the smaller root (near intersection). Good.
- Hence discriminant = B^2 - 4AC (standard). And code computes s3 such that `b.mi` to miss when s3 < 0 and uses `fsqrt(s3)` as sqrt(disc). So s3 must equal +disc. Let's re-evaluate sign with `fnmsub`: If FNMSUB = -(Sn*Sm) - Sa, then s3 = -(B^2) - s3_prev. s3_prev = 4A*(r^2-|o-c|^2) = -4AC. Then s3 = -B^2 - (-4AC) = 4AC - B^2. That's negative disc. But then sqrt(s3) invalid. So FNMSUB must be `-(Sn*Sm) + Sa` = Sa - Sn*Sm. Then s3 = s3_prev - B^2 = -4AC - B^2. Still not standard.

Wait let's recall FNMSUB actual definition. In ARMv8: `FNMSUB Vd, Vn, Vm, Va` computes `(-(Vn*Vm)) - Va`? Hmm. Let me search memory: AArch64 floating-point multiply-add instructions:
- FMADD: d = n*m + a
- FMSUB: d = n*m - a
- FNMADD: d = -(n*m) + a
- FNMSUB: d = -(n*m) - a  → i.e., d = -(n*m + a)
So s3 = -(B*B) - (-4AC) = -B^2 + 4AC = 4AC - B^2 = -(B^2 - 4AC) = -disc.
Then branch `b.mi 400f90` if s3 < 0 => if -disc < 0 => disc > 0 => HIT, but branch goes to miss path? That would be wrong; unless branch condition is reversed (mi = greater than?). Let's settle FP condition codes.

FP compare sets flags NZCV based on comparison. Condition suffixes:
- `eq` = equal
- `ne` = not equal
- `lt` = less than
- `le` = less than or equal
- `gt` = greater than
- `ge` = greater than or equal
- `mi` = minus? In ARM, `mi` means result is negative i.e., first < second (for `fcmp Sn, Sm`). Also `pl` means plus i.e., first > second. Actually I recall from ARMv8 assembly: `b.mi` = "branch if minus" (negative), and `b.pl` = "branch if plus" (positive). For `fcmpe Sn, #0.0`, `b.mi` branches if Sn < 0, `b.pl` if Sn > 0.

So at 400eb0 if s3<0 -> miss. If s3 = -disc, misses when disc>0, which is wrong. Unless the discriminant formula differs.

Let's look at 400ea8-400eac again:
```
400ea0: fsub? wait
400ea8: 1e242884 fadd s4, s4, s4      // B = 2*dot
400eac: 1f248c83 fnmsub s3, s4, s4, s3
```
Maybe `fnmsub` here = Sa - Sn*Sm (i.e., "negated multiply-subtract" could mean `-(Sn*Sm - Sa)` = Sa - Sn*Sm)? Let's check ARM docs exactly: 
- `FNMADD`: `d = -(n × m) + a`
- `FMSUB`: `d = n × m − a`
- `FNMSUB`: `d = −(n × m) − a`? 

Actually from ARMv8-A Reference:
- FMADD: Vd = Va + Vn × Vm
- FMSUB: Vd = Va - Vn × Vm  (multiply-subtract)
- FNMADD: Vd = -Va + Vn × Vm? Hmm.

Let's not rely on memory; we can empirically determine by writing a mini aarch64 assembly program? Or use objdump to decompile known code: In shellcode we can compile C with formulas and compare disassembly. Easiest: write a tiny C function doing `d = b*b - disc` and see instructions. But here it's a single instruction `fnmsub s3, s4, s4, s3`. Let's search glibc source or compile test.

Let's create a small C file `t.c`:
```
float f(float a,float b){ return a*a - b; }
```

Great! So `fnmsub s0,s0,s0,s1` computes `a*a - b`. Explanation: In ARM, FNMSUB d = Sn*Sm - Sa? Wait test: f(a,b) returns a*a - b, and GCC emitted `fnmsub s0,s0,s0,s1`. So FNMSUB computes Sn*Sm - Sa (without negation!). Interesting. Actually the "negated" refers to subtract order? Let's verify: g(b - a*a) emitted fmsub. f(a*a - b) emitted fnmsub. So:
- `fnmsub s3,s4,s4,s3` = s4*s4 - s3.
Great! So s3 = B^2 - (4A*(r^2-|o-c|^2)) = B^2 + 4A*(|o-c|^2 - r^2) = B^2 - 4AC. That's standard discriminant. 

Let's also map other instructions:
- `fmsub s0,s3,s3,s0` then = s3*s3 - s0 = r^2 - |o-c|^2. Yes.
- At 400eac now s3 = s4*s4 - s3_prev = B^2 - 4A*(r^2 - C? wait s3_prev=4A*(r^2-|o|^2)=4A*(-C) = -4AC. So B^2 - (-4AC) = B^2+4AC? Wait compute: s0 = r^2 - |o-c|^2 = -C. s3_prev = 4A*s0 = -4AC. Then s3 = B^2 - s3_prev = B^2 + 4AC. That's not standard disc B^2 - 4AC. Unless C is defined as (r^2-|o-c|^2). Let's recompute standard with C = r^2 - |o-c|^2? Standard: quadratic A t^2 + B t + C = 0 where C = |o-c|^2 - r^2. But code uses s0 = r^2 - |o-c|^2 = -C. So s3 = B^2 + 4A*s0 = B^2 - 4AC. Good! Because 4A*s0 = 4A*(-C) = -4AC. So s3 = B^2 - 4AC. Perfect.

So sphere_intersect:
- A = |d|^2
- B = 2 d·(o-c)
- C = |o-c|^2 - r^2
- disc = B^2 - 4AC
- if disc < 0 miss.
- t = (-B - sqrt(disc))/(2A)
Great.

The ASCII shows a big sphere/ball center with red "R" edges, drawn over checkerboard floor, with a dark shadow. Looks like a ray-traced sphere with red-ish tint and checkerboard floor maybe.

Our goal: produce identical image. We can implement the assembly algorithm in C with float32 semantics. Since exact matching matters, we need replicate floating-point operations in same order/rounding. Writing C with `float` and same expression order should match closelyable platform same aarch64. But we must reconstruct precisely.

Let's write a Python prototype to discover the algorithm and compare bytes. Start from the disassembly.

Let me formalize main algorithm based on current understanding.

Per row `r` (0..599):
- z0 = 1 - r/599.0f
- Print progress.
- a = 2*z0
- For col c (0..799):
  - u = c / 799.0f
  - rx = (u + 2.0f)*z0 - 1.0f
  - ry = u*a - 4.0f/3.0f
  - rz = u*z0 - 1.0f
  - L = sqrt(rx^2+ry^2+rz^2)
  - dx = ry/L, dy = rx/L, dz = rz/L
  - ray1 = (origin (0,0,z0), dir (dx,dy,dz))
  - sphere1 = ((z0, -0.5, -5), radius 1)
  - hit1 = sphere_intersect(ray1, sphere1)
  - if !hit1 or |hit1.normal.x| < eps -> (background: if hit? actually if hit with small normal.x goes to 400a98; if miss -> sky)
     Branch behavior:
       - miss (hit1=0): normal.x=0 => |normal.x|<eps => branch 400a60 -> since w27=0 -> sky color (400a64).
       - hit1=1 and |normal.x|<eps: branch 400a60 -> since w27=1 -> 400a98.
       - hit1=1 and |normal.x|>=eps: continue.
  - t? = -1.5 / normal.x
    - if t < 0.001 -> branch 400a94 -> if hit (always hit here) -> 400a98.
    - else (t >= eps):
       compute plane point (something) then branch on normal.z? Actually:
       if hit -> 400ad0 which compares normal.z > hit[0]? If true -> 400a98 else -> 400ad8.
- Path 400ad8 (w27=0): represent "plane/floor"? Let's map its state to colors:
  - after this path, the final color for no-shadow (hit2=0) is `color = dot*0.8+0.2` then w27=0 -> checkerboard multiply.
  - For shadow (hit2=1): s0=0.2, then checkerboard.
  Checkerboard: `factor = (parity odd ? 0.9 : 0.4)` based on floor(s4+s15) where s4=[sp]=hit.x, s15=??? In path 400ad8, what's s15? Let's determine from state:
    At 400ad8 (entry from 400ad0 false):
      - s7 = s14 = hit.x
      - s17 = s15 (ray dz, since s15 unchanged)
      - s18 = s4 (where s4 was computed at 400854 = s13*t + s14 = hit.y*t + hit.x)
      - s13 = s14 = hit.x
      - s8 = s9 = ray dz
      - w27 = 0
    Then common code 400878:
      - It stores `stp s4,s7,[sp]`: but at this point s4 is still what? s4 was set at 400854 and not modified in 400ad8. So sp[0]=s4=hit.y*t+hit.x, sp[4]=s7=hit.x.
      - It computes s5=...; s17 stores etc.
    Later after second sphere:
      - At 4008e8 `ldr s4,[sp]` => s4 = sp[0] = hit.y*t + hit.x.
      - If no shadow and w27=0, goes to 400a08 which uses s4 (this value) and s15. What is s15 at that point? At 400ad8, s15 was NOT modified (s15=ray dz). Then common code 400878 doesn't modify s15? Let's check 400878: it uses s15? It doesn't assign s15. At 4008e0 sphere_intersect may modify s15? No sphere doesn't modify s15. So s15 remains ray dz. Thus checker coords = floor(s4 + s15) where s4 = hit.y*t + hit.x and s15 = dz.
  Hmm weird but okay.

- Path 400a98 (w27=1): sphere lighting with normal and hit point:
  - This path is taken when:
    - hit1 and |nx|<eps
    - or hit1 and (t < eps or normal.z > s16)
  - state:
    - n = sphere normal (nx,ny,nz)
    - p = hit point (px,py,pz)
    - ray2 origin = p + n*0.001
    - ray2 dir = ??? Let's determine from code:
      At path 400a98:
      - s13 = nx
      - s8 = ny
      - s7 = nz
      - s17 = 0.001
      - s0 = p.y (hit.y)
      - s4 = p.x
      - s15 = p.z
      - s1 = ny*0.001
      - s18 = nx*0.001 + p.x
      - s8 = dz * ny   (s8 becomes dz*ny)
      - s17 = nz*0.001 + p.z
      - w27=1
      Then 400878:
      - s5 = s9^2+s11^2+s10^2 = 1
      - s16 = s1 + s0 = ny*eps + p.y (origin.y)
      - s5 = s9*s9 + s11*s11 + s10*s10; s17 = s11/L = dy, s16 = s9/L = dz, s5=s10/L=dx
      - stores:
        sp[160]=s18 (origin.x)
        sp[164]=s16 (origin.y)
        sp[168]=s17 (origin.z)
      Wait sp[168]=s17 was overwritten? At 400884 `str s17,[sp,#168]` happens after 400878 before 400894. In 400878, s17 got set to s11/L (dy) at 4008bc, then stored at sp[168]. So origin.z = dy (not nz*eps+p.z)! Because the store at 400884 stores the NEW s17 = dy. The old s17 (nz*eps+p.z) is overwritten before storing. Let's recheck timing:

      At 400878:
      ```
      400878: fmul s5, s9, s9
      40087c: fadd s16, s1, s0       // s16 = origin.y = ny*eps + p.y
      400880: fmadd s5, s11, s11, s5
      400884: str s17, [sp,#168]      // stores CURRENT s17 (from path) BEFORE it is overwritten? When is s17 overwritten? s17 is overwritten at 4008bc (later). So at 400884, s17 is still path's s17 = nz*eps+p.z. Good.
      40088c: stp s4, s7, [sp]        // s4=p.x, s7=nz
      400890: movi v0.2s,#0
      400894: stp s18, s16, [sp,#160] // s18=origin.x, s16=origin.y
      400898: fmov s1,#-0.5
      40089c: fmadd s5, s10,s10,s5
      4008a0: ldr q4...
      4008a4: fmov s2,#-5
      4008a8: fmov s3,#1
      4008ac: mov x0,x19
      4008b0: add x8,sp,#0xd8
      4008b4: str q4,[sp,#112]
      4008b8: fsqrt s5,s5
      4008bc: fdiv s17, s11, s5     // s17 = s11/L = dy
      4008c0: fdiv s16, s9, s5      // s16 = s9/L = dz
      4008c4: fdiv s5, s10, s5      // s5 = s10/L = dx
      4008c8: stp s17,s16,[sp,#172] // stores dy,dz at sp[172..179]
      4008cc: ldp x2,x3,[sp,#160]
      4008d0: str s5,[sp,#180]      // stores dx at sp[180]
      4008d4: ldr x1,[sp,#176]      // reads dz,dx for dir.z? 
      4008d8: stp x2,x3,[x19]
      4008dc: str x1,[x19,#16]
      4008e0: bl sphere_intersect
      ```
      So sp[168] = old s17 (nz*eps+p.z) is stored before being overwritten. So ray2 origin = (s18, s16, old s17) = (nx*eps+p.x, ny*eps+p.y, nz*eps+p.z). Good.

      ray2 dir = (s17 at 172, s16 at 176, s5 at 180) = (s11/L, s9/L, s10/L) = (dy, dz, dx). Where s11,s9,s10 are dir from first ray: s11=dy, s9=dz, s10=dx. So dir2 = (dy, dz, dx). Weird but okay. Maybe this is not a shadow ray but a "reflection" direction formed by permuting components? Could be a bug in our register assumption about which slot holds which ray dir. Let's double-check ray struct layout and load in sphere_intersect: ray struct [o0,o1,o2,d0,d1,d2].
      - main builds first ray: at 4007fc etc stores s13(dx) to sp[148], s8(dy) to sp[152], s15(dz) to sp[156].
      - Thus bytes: sp[136]=o0=0, sp[140]=o1=0, sp[144]=o2=z0, sp[148]=d0=dx=s13, sp[152]=d1=dy=s8, sp[156]=d2=dz=s15.
      - sphere_intersect loads d0=s10=dx, d1=s11=dy, d2=s9=dz alert earlier mapping: At 400e70 `ldp s12,s10,[x0,#8]` reads bytes 8..15 = o2,z0 and d0=dx. So s10=d0=dx. At 400e6c `ldp s11,s9,[x0,#16]` reads bytes16..23 = d1=dy, d2=dz => s11=dy, s9=dz. Yes.
      So s10=dx, s11=dy, s9=dz.

      In path 400a98, they set `s8 = s9 * s8` (dz * ny) maybe for later diffuse calculation, not dir2.
      At common 400878, dir2 = (s11/s5, s9/s5, s10/s5) = (dy, dz, dx). So indeed a cyclic permutation. That seems like a bug but it's what binary doesustain. Maybe the intended dir2 is "light direction" which equals `(dy,dz,dx)`? No.

Actually maybe path 400a98 corresponds to the plane (floor), not the sphere! Let's reconsider. The second ray may be a "shadow ray to light" from the floor hit point. The floor plane intersection might produce hit point coordinates and "normal" perhaps equal to (dx,dy,dz)? Hmm.

Let's write Python prototype.

Definitions:
```
import struct, math

def f32(x):
    return struct.unpack('>f', struct.pack('>f', x))[0]
```

sphere_intersect(o,d,c,r):
```
ox,oy,oz=o; dx,dy,dz=d; cx,cy,cz=c
A = dx*dx+dy*dy+dz*dz
oc = (ox-cx, oy-cy, oz-cz)
B = 2*(dx*ocx+dy*ocy+dz*ocz)
C = ocx*ocx+ocy*ocy+ocz*ocz - r*r
disc = B*B - 4*A*C
if disc < 0: miss
sq = sqrt(disc)
t = (-B - sq)/(2*A)
if t < 0? sphere_intersect also checks t vs eps? Let's recall it checks t against 0.001 threshold? At sphere_intersect after computing t:
- 400f10 fcmpe s5,s0 where s0=0.001; `b.mi 400fcc` if t < eps -> use other root? Actually in hit path:
  - t = (-B - sqrt)/(2A)
  - if t < 0.001: branch 400fcc which recomputes using +sqrt? Let's look.
```
In sphere_intersect, after computing t_small = (-B - sqrt)/(2A), it checks if t < 0.001. If yes, goes to 400fcc which computes t_large = (-B + sqrt)/(2A) and checks if t_large < 0.001 then miss else return hit with t_large. So sphere_intersect returns nearest t >= 0.001, else miss.
Specifically:
- 400f10: fcmpe s5,s0 (s0=0.001); b.mi 400fcc (if t<eps)
- 400fcc: sq = sqrt(disc) again; d4 = -B double; d3 = sqrt; d4 = d4 + d3; t = d4/(2A). Then if t < 0.001 -> miss (400f90), else hit.
So threshold 0.001 avoid returning intersections too close to origin.

Thus sphere_intersect:
```
disc = B*B - 4*A*C
if disc < 0: miss
rt = sqrt(disc)
t1 = (-B - rt)/(2*A)
if t1 < 0.001:
    t2 = (-B + rt)/(2*A)
    if t2 < 0.001: miss
    else t=t2
else t=t1
```
Return hit with point, normal.

[tool-b82] exec_command <- Brute force sphere params to match red mask ($ cat > /tmp/brute_sph.py <<'PY' import math def sph(o,d,c,r,tol=0.001): ox,oy,oz=o; dx,dy,dz=d; cx,cy,cz=c A=dx*dx+dy*dy+dz*dz lx=ox-cx; ly=oy-cy; lz=oz-cz B=2*(dx*lx+dy*ly+dz*lz) C=lx*lx+ly*ly+lz*lz-r*r disc=B*B-4*A*C if disc<0: return None rt=math.sqrt(disc); den=2*A t1=(-B-rt)/den if t1 < tol: t2=(-B+rt)/den if t2 < tol: return None t=t2 else: t=t1 return t def stats(cx,cy,cz,r): cnt=0; xs=[]; ys=[] for r_ in range(600): z0=1-r_/599.0; a=2*z0 for c_ in range(800): u=c_/799.0 rx=(u+2)*z0-1; ry=u*a-4/3; rz=u*z0-1 L=math.sqrt(rx*rx+ry*ry+rz*rz) dx,dy,dz=ry/L,rx/L,rz/L if sph((0,0,z0),(dx,dy,dz),(cx,cy,cz),r) is not None: cnt+=1; xs.append(c_); ys.append(r_) if cnt: print((cx,cy,cz,r),'cnt',cnt,'bbox x',min(xs),max(xs),'y',min(ys),max(ys)) return cnt target=(339,460,270,392,11812) # The red bbox roughly x 339-460 -> width 121, y 270-392 -> height 122, cnt 11812 # Search cy=-0.5, cz around -2..0, cx 0..1, r 0.5..2 cands=[] for cx in [x/10 for x in range(0,16)]: for cz in [x/10 for x in range(-30,6)]: for r in [x/10 for x in range(5,26)]: # approximate: ray for center pixel (row 330, col400) hits sphere r_=330; z0=1-r_/599.0; a=2*z0; u=400/799.0 rx=(u+2)*z0-1; ry=u*a-4/3; rz=u*z0-1 L=math.sqrt(rx*rx+ry*ry+rz*rz); dx,dy,dz=ry/L,rx/L,rz/L # distance from sphere center to ray: skip if far # quick: compute closest approach o=(0,0,z0); ocx=o[0]-cx; ocy=o[1]-cy; ocz=o[2]-cz proj=dx*ocx+dy*ocy+dz*ocz qx=o[0]-dx*proj; qy=o[1]-dy*proj; qz=o[2]-dz*proj dist=math.sqrt((qx-cx)**2+(qy-cy)**2+(qz-cz)**2) if dist < r: cnt=stats(cx,cy,cz,r) if cnt>0: break print('done') PY python3 /tmp/brute_sph.py)

Let's look at the dead functions `trace`, `is_in_shadow`, `plane_intersect`, `sky_color` to understand intended original code. These might reveal the high-level scene. Since main seems to be an inlined version of similar logic, the helper functions may give hints on formulas (e.g., sphere center, plane).

Let's disassemble `plane_intersect` and `trace` to understand.

`plane_intersect(x0=plane?, s0=ray origin y?, x8=out)`:
- It reads `s2 = [x0,#16]` (plane normal y?)
- s1 = eps constant 4e-5? `0x38d1b717 = 0.0001` (same as earlier)
- if |s2| < eps -> miss
- s3 = [x0,#4] (plane point y?)
- s1 = 0.001
- s0 = (s0 - s3)/s2   // ray intersect t for plane y = plane.y?
- if t < 0.001 miss
- compute hit point:
  s3 = s0*s2 + s3   (y)
  s4 = [x0] + s0*[x0,#8]  (x)
  s2 = [x0,#8] + s0*[x0,#20] (z)
- output plane normal (0,1,0), hit point, flag=1.
So plane struct: {vec3 point, vec3 normal?} maybe {point.x, point.y, point.z, normal.x, normal.y, normal.z}? It uses offsets 0,4,16? Actually plane struct layout:
- [x0] = point.x
- [x0+4] = point.y
- [x0+16] = normal.y (or direction?); [x0+8] and [x0+20] used to compute x,z from t. This is a plane definition with normal n=(?,?).

`trace(ray)` likely does the full shading for a ray, including plane_intersect with plane y=-1.5, sphere_intersect, etc. Let's decode trace; it likely mirrors main's logic and reveals intended values.

Let's disassemble trace 4011c0-4014b0 fully (we already have partial). Let's annotate.

The trace function is essentially the same algorithm but generalized to a ray. It's unused. The main is an inlined version of trace, and they almost match instruction-by-instruction. Let's compare main's inline logic to trace's logic to infer correct mapping.

In trace:
- Input ray x19 (Ray*). It copies to stack ray at sp+16 and calls sphere_intersect at 401228.
- At 40122c: ldr s8,[x19,#16] = ray.dy (offset 16).
- loads s3,[x19,#20] = ray.dz.
- loads s13,s4,[x19,#8] = ray.origin.z, ray.dir.x? Wait x19 struct: [o0,o1,o2,d0,d1,d2]. offset 8=o2, offset 12? Actually:
  - `ldp s13,s4,[x19,#8]` loads two floats at bytes 8..15 => s13=o2, s4=d0.
  - `ldp s12,s1,[x19]` loads at 0..7 => s12=o0, s1=o1.
  - `ldp s7,s5,[sp,#120]` (from sphere_intersect hit struct sp+120?).
So trace at 401240:
  - s13 = o.z
  - s4 = d.x
  - s12 = o.x
  - s1 = o.y
  - s3 = ray.dz
  - s8 = ray.dy
  - w21 = hit flag from [sp,#148].
  - It checks |s8| < eps; if true goto 40144c (miss/sky).
  - else:
    - s0 = (-1.5 - s1)/s8 = (-1.5 - o.y)/dy   // plane y=-1.5 intersection!
    - if t < 0.001 goto 4013b4.
    - else compute plane point:
      - s12 = o.x + t*d.x
      - s1 = o.y + t*dy? wait 401284 `fmadd s1,s0,s8,s1` => s1 = o.y + t*s8 (dy)
      - s13 = o.z + t*raydz? 401288 `fmadd s13,s0,s3,s13` => s13 = o.z + t*dz
      - cbnz w21 (sphere hit?) If sphere hit -> 401480 else continue.
Now trace has a plane floor intersection: `t = (-1.5 - o.y)/dy`. If ray hits floor plane, compute floor hit point. If sphere also hit? It branches based on w21 (sphere hit flag).

This matches main? In main, after sphere_intersect, s8 = normal.x (if hit) or 0 if miss Rosin. But in trace, s8 is loaded from ray.dy BEFORE sphere_intersect? Wait at 4011ec it loads s8 = [x19,#16] = ray.dy BEFORE call, and keeps it. Then after sphere_intersect, s8 may be overwritten by the function! But trace at 40122c does `ldr s8,[x19,#16]` AFTER the sphere_intersect call (401228). Since sphere_intersect may clobber s8 (in hit path, s8 becomes normal.x; in miss path, s8=0). So after the call, s8 = normal.x or 0 in trace too. Then the plane intersection uses `s0 = (-1.5 - s1)/s8` where s1 = o.y (loaded after call from x19, not touched by sphere_intersect? sphere_intersect may modify s1? In hit path it sets s1=normal.y; in miss path s1=0. Wait trace loads s1 at 401248 `ldp s12,s1,[x19]` AFTER sphere call, so s1 = ray origin y? The ldp loads ray origin from memory x19, so s1 is o.y, not register s1 leftover. So it re-reads origin from memory. Good.)
So trace uses s8 (which after sphere_intersect is normal.x if hit else 0) to compute floor intersection t = (-1.5 - o.y)/s8. That's the same weird formula as main. Then it computes plane point using ray dir fetched from memory; the branch w21 decides sphere vs floor.

This confirms the algorithm: if sphere hit (w21=1), normal.x used as denominator to find "floor intersection" (calling it m?); if sphere not hit, s8=0 leads to sky.

Wait in trace, if sphere miss, s8=0, |0|<eps => branch to sky. So the floor only rendered when sphere hit? That seems odd for a scene with floor under sphere. But maybe the floor is only rendered inside the sphere's disk? Let's look at image: floor spans entire bottom, not just inside sphere. So floor cannot be only rendered when sphere hit. Hmm.

Actually maybe w21 is not "sphere hit" but something else. Let's identify the call in trace: It calls sphere_intersect at 401228 with ray at sp+16. Which sphere? We need see what center/radius passed. At 401228 call, s0,s1,s2,s3? The code before:
- 4011ec ldr s8,[x19,#16]
- 4011f0 ldp x4,x5,[x19] load ray origin

What are s0,s1,s2,s3 at call? They could be incoming arguments to trace? Actually trace signature maybe `trace(Ray *ray, float s0,float s1,float s2,float s3)`? Perhaps trace's s0,s1,s2,s3 correspond to `sphere_intersect(ray, center, radius)`, same as main's first call. In main, s0=z0, s1=-0.5, s2=-5, s3=1. So sphere center=(z0,-0.5,-5), radius=1, consistent.

But then floor plane? In trace, after sphere intersect, it computes `(-1.5 - o.y)/s8` where s8 is normal.x (if hit) or 0. If hit, this is not a floor intersection with ray direction; it's using the sphere normal x to compute something else. Hmm.

Actually wait: after sphere_intersect, `s8` is not necessarily normal.x. In trace, maybe sphere_intersect returns distance in s8 not normal? Let's re-examine sphere_intersect output register mapping by looking at how trace uses s8. At 40122c (after call), s8 loaded from `ldr s8,[x19,#16]` which is the ORIGINAL ray.dy from memory! Ah! At 40122c it re-loads `s8` from the ray struct at offset 16, which is ray.dir.y. So s8 = ray.dy, NOT the sphere normal. The sphere_intersect call may have clobbered s8 register, but trace reloads it from memory. So in trace, s8 = original ray.dy! Then t = (-1.5 - o.y)/dy is genuinely the plane y=-1.5 intersection!

Similarly, main at 40081c does `fabs s0, s8` but s8 is? Let's check if main reloaded s8 from ray struct memory after first sphere call. At 400804 before call, main constructed ray and stored components into sp[148..155]; after sphere call, does it reload s8 from memory or use register leftover?

Main after sphere call at 40081c:
```
40081c: 1e20c100 fabs s0, s8
```
This uses s8 register directly, NOT re-loaded from memory. But was s8 loaded from memory before the call? Yes at 4007fc `fdiv s8, s8, s5` computed dy and stored to sp[152]. s8 register contains dy. Then sphere_intersect is called, which in hit path overwrites s8 with normal.x; in miss path sets s8=0. So s8 after call is NOT the original dy. UNLESS sphere_intersect is compiled to preserve s8? It uses s8 internally, so it will be clobbered. Thus main's `|s8|` is `|normal.x|` if hit else 0 Satisfies. Whereas trace reloads s8 from memory after call, yielding dy. The two functions differ! So main's inline version is NOT identical to trace. The unused trace is a separate, possibly earlier version with plane intersection.

Therefore our main analysis stands: first sphere hit result's normal.x is used in a weird waychas.

But maybe we misread main's stack layout: after sphere call, main does NOT reload dx/dy/dz from memory; it continues using registers from sphere_intersect. The path after first sphere uses:
- s8 = normal.x (if hit)
- s14 = hit.x
- s13 = hit.y
- s12 = hit.z
- s15 = ray dz? (not modified)
- s9,s10,s11 = ray dir (not modified by sphere_intersect? wait sphere_intersect uses s9,s10,s11, but should not modify them, so they remain ray dx,dy,dz)

This matches our earlier reading.

So main's logic is:
- Cast primary ray.
- If it hits a sphere (center=(z0,-0.5,-5)), then compute something with normal.x and hit point.
- Otherwise sky/floor? But how does the floor appear if not via sphere hit? The floor in the image is huge, so there must be another path that computes floor when sphere miss.

Let's look at the main branch structure again. Suppose sphere miss -> w27=0, s8=0, branch at 400830 `|0|<eps` -> 400a60 -> cbnz w27 false -> sky color path 400a64. That would make the entire bottom floor sky, which contradicts image.

Unless the sphere is NOT the red sphere; maybe the main's first sphere is the floor? Wait the floor might be rendered by the second sphere_intersect! Let's consider: perhaps the first sphere_intersect is a "sphere mirror/horizon" and the floor is computed when sphere miss via a different code path (400a64) that isn't sky but floor. [tool-59e] exec_command <- Brute force sphere to match red mask directly ($ cat > /tmp/brute_red.py <<'PY' import math, time # Precompute rays rays=[] for r in range(600): z0=1-r/599.0; a=2*z0 for c in range(800): u=c/799.0 rx=(u+2)*z0-1; ry=u*a-4/3; rz=u*z0-1 L=math.sqrt(rx*rx+ry*ry+rz*rz) rays.append(((0,0,z0),(ry/L,rx/L,rz/L))) # actual mask red data=open('/app/image.ppm').read().split() W,H,Max=int(data[1]),int(data[2]),int(data[3]) vals=list(map(int,data[4:])) actual=[] for y in range(H): for x in range(W): o=(y*W+x)*3; r,g,b=vals[o:o+3] actual.append(1 if (r>50 and r>g*1.5 and r>b*1.5) else 0) print('actual red count',sum(actual)) def hitsphere(cx,cy,cz,r): mask=[] ocx0=-cx; ocy0=-cy; ocz0=-cz for idx,((ox,oy,oz),(dx,dy,dz)) in enumerate(rays): lx=ox-cx; ly=oy-cy; lz=oz-cz B=2*(dx*lx+dy*ly+dz*lz) C=lx*lx+ly*ly+lz*lz-r*r disc=B*B-4*C hit=False if disc>=0: rt=math.sqrt(disc) t1=(-B-rt)/2 if t1>=0.001: hit=True else: t2=(-B+rt)/2 if t2>=0.001: hit=True mask.append(1 if hit else 0) return mask def compare(mask): tp=sum(1 for a,m in zip(actual,mask) if a and m) fp=sum(1 for a,m in zip(actual,mask) if not a and m) fn=sum(1 for a,m in zip(actual,mask) if a and not m) return tp,fp,fn, len(actual) # coarse grid best=None cy=-0.5 t0=time.time() for cz in [i/4 for i in range(-24,8)]: for cx in [i/4 for i in range(-4,13)]: for r in [i/4 for i in range(2,10)]: mask=hitsphere(cx,cy,cz,r) tp,fp,fn,tot=compare(mask) score=fp+fn if best is None or score<best[0]: best=(score,tp,fp,fn,(cx,cy,cz,r)) print('best',best,'time',time.time()-t0) print('FINAL',best) PY timeout 300 python3 /tmp/brute_red.py)

- pixel struct: 3 floats at [x4 + x19], [x3+4], [x3+8].

- s1 = [x3+4] (offset 4)

Hmm, wait. Let me reconsider: maybe main's store is (s0, s1, s8) but in write_image they're read in different order due to endiannes/register pairing. Let me re-check write_image more carefully.

write_image inner loop:
```
401550: ldr x4, [x20]           ; x4 = row pointer (current row's pixel array)
40155c: add x3, x4, x19         ; x3 = pixel_ptr + offset
401560: add x3, x4, x19         ; (confusing but ok)
401564: ldr s2, [x4, x19]       ; s2 = pixel[0]
401568: add x19, x19, #0xc      ; advance offset by 12
40156c: ldp s1, s0, [x3, #4]    ; s1 = [x3+4], s0 = [x3+8]
```

So printed = pixel[0] (s2), pixel[1] (s1), pixel[2] (s0).

main store:
```
400944: stp s0, s1, [x20]       ; x20[0]=s0, x20[1]=s1
400948: add x20, x20, #12
40094c: stur s8, [x20, #-4]    ; [x20_new-4] = [x20_old+8] = s8
```
x20[0]=s0, x20[4]=s1, x20[8]=s8. Printed as (s0, s1, s8).

OK so my assumption stands.

4007cc: ldr s6, [sp, #20]     ; s6 = 8/3
4007d0: fmadd s6, s4, s6, s12 ; s6 = u*(8/3) + s12. What is s12?
```
s12: at 0x400754 `movi v12.2s, #0` → v12 = (0,0). At 0x400774 `fmul s12, s14, s12` → s12 = z0 * (0,0) = (0,0). So s12 = 0. So s6 = u*8/3.

Wait, but s12 is a paired register (v12.2s): fmul s12, s14, s12 multiplies the 2S vector by scalar? Actually `fmul s12, s14, s12` operates on S registers (single) = 0 * 0 = 0. Simd pairing only matters for stp/ldp. Fine.

```
4007d4: fmadd s4, s4, s14, s12 ; s4 = u*z0 + 0
4007d8: fsub s8, s8, s5        ; s8 = (u+2)z0 - 1
4007dc: fsub s6, s6, s7        ; s6 = u*8/3 - 4/3
4007e0: fsub s4, s4, s5        ; s4 = u*z0 - 1
```

So rx = (u+2)z0 - 1, ry = (8/3)u - 4/3, rz = u*z0 - 1. Yes, these are components BEFORE normalization (rx, ry, rz).

Then:
```
4007e4: fmul s5, s8, s8    ; s5 = rx²
4007e8: fmadd s5, s6, s6, s5 ; s5 += ry²
4007ec: fmadd s5, s4, s4, s5 ; s5 += rz²
4007f0: fsqrt s5, s5        ; L
4007f4: fdiv s13, s6, s5   ; s13 = ry/L
4007f8: fdiv s8, s8, s5    ; s8 = rx/L
4007fc: fdiv s15, s4, s5   ; s15 = rz/L
```

So dir = (rx/L, ry/L, rz/L) assigned to (s8, s13, s15). Then:

- MI: minus (negative)

- MI (less than): N=1

Actually in AArch64 the condition mnemonics for fcmpe are: EQ, NE, GE, LT, GT, LE, VS (unordered), LO, LS, HI, HS, PL, MI.

`b.mi` = branch if N flag set → "less than" (or -0 < +0 etc.). For fcmpe with NaN, unordered makes N=1?? Actually I recall: for `fcmp` instructions, the condition flags are:
- N: set if the result is less than, or if either operand is NaN (unordered → N=1).
- Z: set if equal.

[tool-59f] exec_command <- Analytic geometry classification ($ python3 - <<'EOF' import math, struct import numpy as np W,H=800,600 def ray_intersect_sphere(ox,oy,oz, dx,dy,dz, cx,cy,cz, r): # returns (hit, t, nx,ny,nz) sx = ox-cx; sy=oy-cy; sz=oz-cz a = dx*dx+dy*dy+dz*dz b = 2*(sx*dx+sy*dy+sz*dz) c = sx*sx+sy*sy+sz*sz-r*r disc = b*b-4*a*c if disc < 0: return (0,0,0,0,0) sq = math.sqrt(disc) # sphere_intersect computes t = (-b - sqrt(disc))/(2a) (nearest) t = (-b - sq)/(2*a) if t < 0.001: return (0,0,0,0,0) px = ox+t*dx; py=oy+t*dy; pz=oz+t*dz nx = (px-cx)/r; ny=(py-cy)/r; nz=(pz-cz)/r return (1,t,px,py,pz,nx,ny,nz) def analyze(): hit1_counts = 0 classes = {} for r in range(H): z0 = 1 - r/599.0 a = 2*z0 for c in range(W): u = c/799.0 rx = (u+2)*z0 - 1 ry = u*(8/3.0) - (4/3.0) rz = u*z0 - 1 L = math.sqrt(rx*rx+ry*ry+rz*rz) dx = ry/L # careful: direction stored as (ry/L, rx/L, rz/L) dy = rx/L dz = rz/L res = ray_intersect_sphere(0,0,z0, dx,dy,dz, z0,-0.5,-5, 1.0) hit1 = res[0] nx = res[6] if hit1: hit1_counts += 1 key = 'hit' else: key = 'miss' classes[key] = classes.get(key,0)+1 print("hit1:", hit1_counts, "miss:", H*W-hit1_counts) analyze() EOF)

Actually let me define what sphere_intersect does precisely:

```
int sphere_intersect(ray *r, vec3 c, float radius, Hit *hit)
{
    float ox=r->o[0], oy=r->o[1], oz=r->o[2];
    float dx=r->d[0], dy=r->d[1], dz=r->d[2];
    float s8; // normal.x return is computed from s8 entry
    ...
}
```

So sphere_intersect(ray, center, radius, hit) is a normal function:
```
int sphere_intersect(ray *r, vec3 c, float radius, Hit *hit)
{
    // cx = c.x in s8; cy=c.y; cz=c.z; radius in s3 initially but later s3 holds other stuff
    float dx=r->d[0], dy=r->d[1], dz=r->d[2];
    float ox=r->o[0], oy=r->o[1], oz=r->o[2];
    float bx = ox-cx, by=oy-cy, bz=oz-cz;
    float a = dx²+dy²+dz²;             // |d|²
    float b = 2*(bx*dx+by*dy+bz*dz);   // 2·(o-c)·d
    float c = bx²+by²+bz² - radius²;   // |o-c|² - r²
    float disc = b*b - 4*a*c;  // (2dot)² - 4|d|²(|o-c|²-r²)
    if (disc < 0) → miss
    float t = (float)((-(double)b - sqrt((double)disc)) / (2.0*(double)a));  // nearest root
    if (t < 0.001) → miss
    hit->point = o + t*d
    float n = sqrt(nx²+ny²+nz²);
    hit->normal = (point - center)/n
    hit->hit = 1
}
```

Let me verify the discriminant sign: The assembly computes s3 = 4|d|²(|o-c|²-r²) - (2dot)². That's = -(disc). Then checks `if disc < 0` via fcmpe s3, #0.0 → `b.mi`: if s3 < 0 → miss. s3 = 4a(c) - b² = -(b²-4ac). s3 < 0 ⟺ b²-4ac > 0 → hit. OK consistent.

Then t = (-b - sqrt(disc))/(2a). In assembly, note b here = 2dot (they did s4 = 2*dot). t = (-b - sqrt(b²-4ac)) / (2a). This is the nearest intersection (smaller root). Good.

```

- 400eb8: fcvt d3, s3  → d3 = (double)s3 where s3 = 4a·c - b² (positive for hit).

- 400ecc: if d3 == 0: d0 = d3 (0)... goes to sqrt(0) path.

- s3_before = 4|d|²(|o-c|²-r²)  [from 400ea0-400ea8]

Hmm, actually I need to be careful about the fcmpe condition again. `fcmpe s3, #0.0` sets FPCR bits. The condition MI (minus) is true when N=1. For FP compare, N=1 when the comparison is less-than OR unordered. So b.mi → branch if s3 < 0 (or NaN). So s3 >= 0 continues (HIT path). s3 = 4ac - b². - The sphere intersection equation: |o + t·d - c|² = r²
- Expanding: |d|² t² + 2·(o-c)·d · t + (|o-c|² - r²) = 0
- So a = |d|², b = 2·(o-c)·d, c = |o-c|² - r².

OK let me resolve this definitively by checking the ARM condition semantics with actual tests. Let me write a tiny assembly program or use gdb to determine b.mi behavior after fcmpe.

Actually, let me look up: in AArch64, `fcmpe` is "Floating-point Compare Equal" — it sets flags based on comparison. The condition mnemonics map to NZCV:
From ARM documentation (Cond field encoding):
- EQ: Z=1
- NE: Z=0
- CS/HS: C=1
- CC/LO: C=0
- MI: N=1
- PL: N=0
- VS: V=1
- VC: V=0
- HI: C=1 && Z=0
- LS: C=0 || Z=1
- GE: N==V
- LT: N!=V
- GT: Z=0 && N==V
- LE: Z=1 || N!=V

For FCMP, the flags are set as if comparing "integer-like" values but with special handling. Actually for FP comparisons on AArch64, the NZCV flags after FCMP are set such that:
- If op1 < op2: N=1, Z=0, C=1, V=0
- If op1 > op2: N=0, Z=0, C=0, V=0
- If op1 == op2: N=0, Z=1, C=1, V=0
- If unordered (NaN): N=1, Z=0, C=1, V=1? 

So MI (N=1) after FCMP = "less than" (including unordered). So `fcmpe s3, #0.0; b.mi` branches if s3 < 0.0.

So my reading stands: the assembly misses when s3 < 0 where s3 = 4ac - b², i.e., misses when b² > 4ac, i.e., misses when D < 0 — wait, D = b² - 4ac. s3 = -D. s3 < 0 ⟺ -D < 0 ⟺ D > 0. So miss when D > 0?

That's backwards from correct math! Unless the "b" in the code is the NEGATIVE of 2dot... let me recompute s4 = 2*dot sign.

s4 dot = (o-c)·d = ((ox-cx)*dx + (oy-cy)*dy + (oz-cz)*dz).
- At 400e7c: s4 = (oy-cy)*dy  (s4* s11 where s4=oy-cy, s11=dy)

So I must have the sign of s3 wrong. Let me re-examine `fnmsub s3, s4, s4, s3`. The fnmsub instruction: `FNMADD`-like· For `fnmsub Vd.2S, Vn.2S, Vm.2S, Va.2S` → Vd = -(Vn × Vm) + Va? Or fnmsub means "Floating-point Negated Multiply-Subtract": result = -(a × b) - c? Hmm.

Let me get this right. The four fused multiply-add variants in ARMv8:
- FMADD: d = a·b + c
- FMSUB: d = a·b - c
- FNMADD: d = -(a·b) + c  = c - a·b
- FNMSUB: d = -(a·b) - c = -a·b - c

`fnmsub s3, s4, s4, s3` — the operand order in ARM syntax is `Fnmsub Vd, Vn, Vm, Va` which computes `Vd = -(Vn × Vm) + Va`? Or `Va - Vn×Vm`?

From ARM Architecture Reference: `FNMSUB` computes `-(Sn * Sm) + Sa`? Let me recall from glibc SIMD: 
- FMLS: d = a·b - c (fused multiply-subtract)
- FNMLA: d = -(a·b) - c
- FNMLS/FNMSUB: d = -(a·b) + c? 

Per ARM ARM (https://developer.arm.com/documentation): 
- FMADD: Vd = Va + Vn × Vm
- FMSUB: Vd = Va − Vn × Vm
- FNMADD: Vd = −(Va + Vn × Vm)
- FNMSUB: Vd = −(Va − Vn × Vm) = Vn×Vm − Va

Hmm, there's ambiguity between instruction name and syntax order. Let me just test empirically with a tiny C/asm experiment on this machine. Actually easier: use gdb to step through sphere_intersect? Or write a tiny C program with inline asm.

So the sphere_intersect is a correct ray-sphere intersection:
```
a = |d|²
b = 2·((o-c)·d)
c = |o-c|² - r²
D = b² - 4ac
if D < 0: miss
t = (-b - sqrt(D)) / (2a)   [using double precision]
if t < 0.001: miss
hit point, normal (normalized), flag=1
```

Let me verify t computation: 
- s15 = 2a (from 400ee8: fadd s15, s15, s15 → s15 = 2|d|²)
- d4 = (double)(-b) — wait: 400ebc: fneg s4, s4 → s4 = -b. Then fcvt d4, s4 (400ec4). 
- d3 = (double)D; if D>0: d3 = sqrt(d3) (400f60)
- d5 = d4 - d3 = -b - sqrt(D)
- d15 = (double)(2a)
- t = d5 / d15 = (-b - sqrt(D))/(2a)
- s5 = (float)t
- if s5 < 0.001 → miss

Yes! Standard nearest-root formula. 

Now the normal: point = o + t·d; norm = (point - center)/|point-center|. And hit flag = 1.

Let me structure:

```c
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
#include <stdint.h>
#include <string.h>
#include <math.h>

typedef struct { float x,y,z; } vec3;
typedef struct ray { float ox,oy,oz,dx,dy,dz; } ray;
typedef struct hit {
    float pad;
    float px,py,pz;
    float nx,ny,nz;
    int hit;
} hit;

static int sphere_intersect(ray *r, vec3 center, float radius, hit *h){
    float ox=r->ox, oy=r->oy, oz=r->oz;
    float dx=r->dx, dy=r->dy, dz=r->dz;
    float cx=center.x, cy=center.y, cz=center.z;
    float a = dx*dx+dy*dy+dz*dz所在的;
    float bx=ox-cx, by=oy-cy, bz=oz-cz;
    float c = bx*bx+by*by+bz*bz - radius*radius;
    float b = 2*(bx*dx+by*dy+bz*dz);
    float disc = b*b - 4*a*c;
    if (disc < 0) { h->hit=0; return 0; }
    double sd = sqrt((double)disc);
    float t = (float)(((double)(-b) - sd) / (2.0*(double)a));
    if (t < 0.001f) { h->hit=0; return 0; }
    float hx = ox + t*dx;
    float hy = oy + t*dy;
    float hz = oz + t*dz;
    float nx = hx-cx, ny=hy-cy, nz=hz-cz;
    float len = sqrtf(nx*nx+ny*ny+nz*nz);
    nx/=len; ny/=len; nz/=len;
    h->pad=0; h->px=hx; h->py=hy; h->pz=hzRuss;
    h->nx=nx; h->ny=ny; h->nz=nz; h->hit=1;
    return 1;
}
```

Wait — careful: the sphere_intersect's float operations need to match EXACTLY (the disc computation etc.) for the hit flags to match. Let me match the operations in the same order as assembly. But the order of float ops affects rounding. Let me replicate the exact order:

Assembly:
1. s0 = (oy-cy)²
2. s15 = dy²
3. s4 = (oy-cy)*dy
4. s0 = (oy-cy)² + (ox-cx)²
5. s6 = oz-cz
6. s15 = dy² + dx²
7. s4 = (oy-cy)*dy + (ox-cx)*dx
8. s0 = |o-c|²
9. s15 = |d|²
10. s4 = dot
11. s0 = |o-c|² - r²   ... 

Hmm, but the compiler would generate the same order if I write C expressions in the right form. Let me write the C to match the exact order:

```c
float omyc = oy-cy;
float omxc = ox-cx;
float omzc = oz-cz;
float a = dy*dy;
a = dx*dx + a;
float b = omyc*dy;
b = omxc*dx + b;
float cc = omzc*cz_... 
```

Actually the assembly order for a:
- s15 = dy*dy
- s15 = dx*dx + s15
- s15 = dz*dz + s15

So a = dz*dz + (dx*dx + dy*dy).

And b (dot):
- s4 = (oy-cy)*dy
- s4 = (ox-cx)*dx + s4
- s4 = (oz-cz)*dz + s4

c (|o-c|²-r²):
- s0 = (oy-cy)²
- s0 = (ox-cx)² + s0   [fmadd s0, s7,s7,s0]
- s0 = (oz-cz)² + s0
- s0 = s0 - r²         [fmsub s0, s3,s3,s0]

disc:
- s3 = 4 * a   [multiplying by 4.0]
- s3 = s3 * c  [fmul s3, s3, s0]
- s4 = 2*b
- s3 = b² - s3  [fnmsub s3, s4, s4, s3 → b² - 4ac]

where note: 4*a*c — the assembly does (4*a)*c? `fmul s3, s15, s5` (s15=a, s5=4) → 4a; then `fmul s3, s3, s0` → (4a)*c. Then + s4 = 2b, then disc = b² - (4a·c). Yes.

Wait actually: dicc check again: s4 = b+b = 2b. disc = (2b)² - 4ac = b²_new... whatever. The formula: with B=2b: disc_actual = B² - 4ac. Assembly: fnmsub s3, s4, s4, s3 → s4² - s3 → (2b)² - 4ac. Good.

Now the double-precision t computation:
- d3 = (double)disc
- if disc > 0: sd = sqrt(d3) (double sqrt)
- else (disc == 0): sd = 0
- t = (double)(-2b) - sd ... wait let me re-derive:

Assembly after disc >= 0:
- 400ebc: s4 = -s4 → s4 = -2b
- d4 = (double)s4 → d4 = -2b (in double)
- if d3 > 0 (disc > 0): d3 = sqrt(d3)
- s15 = 2a (s15 = a+a)
- d5 = d4 - sqrt_disc = -2b - sqrt(disc)
- d15 = (double)(2a)
- t_double = d5/d15 = (-2b - sqrt(disc))/(2a)

Which equals (-b' - sqrt) where b' = 2b = the "B" in standard formula. So t = (-B - sqrt(B²-4ac))/(2a). Correct (nearest root).

- s5 = (float)t
- if s5 < 0.001 → miss. Note: the comparison `fcmpe s5, s0` where s0=0.001 → b.mi → s5 < 0.001 → miss.

The point/normal:
- px = ox + t*dx (t float)
- py = oy + t*dy
- pz = oz + t*dz
- normx = px - cx, normy = py-cy, normz = pz-cz
- len = sqrtf(normx²+normy²+normz²)  [float sqrt]
- nx = normx/len, ny = normy/len, nz = normz/len

Wait — but the assembly computes the point with s13 = s5*s11 + s13 etc. using the FLOAT t (s5), and float dx,dy,dz. So yes.

Also important: the normal normalized via fsqrt (single precision sqrt). In C, sqrtf.

Now let me finish the main logic.

```c
int main(){
    printf("Creating high-resolution image: %dx%d\n", 800, 600);
    fwrite("This will be downsampled with ffmpeg after rendering\n", 1, 53, stdout);
    // note: "Rendering high-resolution image...\n" is written AFTER allocate_image in the assembly,

        float z0f = ... ;

            float ry = u*(8.0f/3.0f) - (4.0f/3.0f);

Now I have a fairly complete picture of the binary. Let me analyze the main function in detail.

The task: Write /app/mystery.c that when compiled with `gcc -static -o reversed mystery.c -lm` and run as `./reversed`, reproduces identical behavior/output (stdout text + image.ppm, P3 800x600) as the AArch64 statically-linked binary /app/mystery.

Also: mystery.c gzip-compressed <2k. So we need a compact C program.

The approach: The main concern is bit-exact reproduction of stdout text and image.ppm. Since we have the reference image /app/image.ppm, and the binary, we need to reconstruct the algorithm to produce the exact same output.

The simplest "self-contained" program that reproduces all behavior would be one that, at runtime, writes the output. We can't just embed the whole 5MB PPM in the source (gzip must be <2k). We need to actually implement the raytracing algorithm.

Let me carefully reverse-engineer main.

Let me define:
- ray origin o=(ox,oy,oz) = (s14,s13,s12)
- ray dir d=(dx,dy,dz) = (s11,s10,s9)
- sphere center c=(s0,s1,s2), radius s3.

Standard ray-sphere: L = o - c; a = d·d; b = 2 (L·d); c = L·L - r². Discriminant = b² - 4ac.

Here:
- s0 = L·L computed: (oy-s1)² + (ox-s0)² + (oz-s2)² — yes s0 accumulates: s4 = oy-cy, s7 = ox-cx, s6 = oz-cz; s0 = s4²+s7²+s6².
- s15 = d·d = dx²+dy²+dz².
- s4 = L·d = (oy-cy)*dx + (ox-cx)*dy + (oz-cz)*dz — computed as s4=s4*s11; s4=s7*s10+s4; s4=s6*s9+s4.
- s0 = s3² - s0 → c = r² - L·L.
- s3 = s15 * 4 → 4a.
- s4 = s4*2 → 2b.
- s3 = s3 * s0 → 4ac.
- s3 = s4*s4 - s3 → b'² - 4ac where b' = 2b, so b'² = 4b². → discriminant = 4(b² - ac). OK.

If discriminant < 0 (fcmp s3 vs 0.0, b.mi → less? b.mi = "b.first"? Actually ".mi" is minus... let me recall: FCMP with condition MI = minus (less than), "b.mi" = branch if less than. Actually the mnemonics: EQ, NE, CS/HS, CC/LO, MI (minus = < 0), PL (plus = > 0). So `fcmpe s3, #0.0; b.mi 400f90` branches to failure if s3 < 0.0. If s3 ≥ 0 (including NaN?
